To fix (2)

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 4 imprecise p values and 1 p value reported as exactly zero out of 8 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
P < 0.05 We assessed predictive performance by five-fold cross-validation, retaining genes imputable in at least one method (CV R² ≥ 0.01, CV P < 0.05) and selecting the model with the highest CV R².
P < 0.05 MR associations were considered significant when a molecular trait passed Bonferroni correction for the number of molecular traits tested (meta-P < 0.05 / N), showed strong causal support with a cumulative posterior inclusion probability (cPIP) of at least 0.7, contained at least one credible set, a…
P > 0.01 MR associations were considered significant when a molecular trait passed Bonferroni correction for the number of molecular traits tested (meta-P < 0.05 / N), showed strong causal support with a cumulative posterior inclusion probability (cPIP) of at least 0.7, contained at least one credible set, a…
P < 0.05 We compared enrichment profiles across datasets using a two-sample z-test on the difference in enrichment, restricted to annotations with P < 0.05.

Cells longer than 300 characters are cut (…).

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

P values are never exactly zero. A p value of .000 is a rounding artifact — the actual value is simply smaller than the reported precision. Very small p values should be reported as p < .001 rather than p = .000. A p = 0 can also be a model parameter, such as an ARIMA order; check that it is a p value.

P-Value Text
P = 0.0 Among neurological diseases, the strongest contribution was observed for Parkinson’s disease 44 (P = 0.0https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?DllOvF0) and Alzheimer’s disease 3 (P = 0.046).

The APA manual states: report p values less than .001 as p < .001.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To check (4)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 1 non-significant p value that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

Text Sentence
P > 0.01 MR associations were considered significant when a molecular trait passed Bonferroni correction for the number of molecular traits tested (meta-P < 0.05 / N), showed strong causal support with a cumulative posterior inclusion probability (cPIP) of at least 0.7, contained at least one credible set, a…

Cells longer than 300 characters are cut (…).

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of misinterpretations of non-significant results is high, see:

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. No absolute file paths were found in any of the code files.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 16 code files, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

Packages / Dependencies

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 26 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

>     plot.title = element_text(face = "bold", size = 8.5, hjust = 0.5, margin = margin(b = 6)),
>     axis.text.x = element_blank(),
>     axis.ticks.x = element_blank(),
>     axis.text.y = element_text(size = 8.2),
>     axis.line = element_line(color = "black", linewidth = 0.35),
>     axis.ticks.y = element_line(color = "black", linewidth = 0.35),
>     axis.title.y = element_text(size = 9.5, margin = margin(r = 3)),
>     plot.margin = margin(2, 5, 2, 5)
>   )
> broken_y_trans <- function(lower = 1800, upper = 6000, compressed_gap = 180) {
>   removed <- upper - lower - compressed_gap
>   scales::trans_new(
>     name = "broken_y",
>     transform = function(x) ifelse(
>       x <= lower, x,
>       ifelse(x >= upper, x - removed, lower + (x - lower) * compressed_gap / (upper - lower))
>     ),
>     inverse = function(x) ifelse(
>       x <= lower, x,
>       ifelse(x >= lower + compressed_gap, x + removed,
>              lower + (x - lower) * (upper - lower) / compressed_gap)
>     )
>   )
> }
> make_d_panel <- function(feature, y_limits, show_y_title = FALSE, broken_axis = FALSE) {
>   p <- counts_per_sample %>%
>     filter(Feature == feature) %>%
>     mutate(
>       x_pos = if (feature == "Glycans") 1.5 else
>         match(GlycanTypeSummary, c("Glycation", "Glycosylation"))
>     ) %>%
>     ggplot(aes(x = x_pos, y = Count, fill = GlycanTypeSummary, group = GlycanTypeSummary)) +
>     geom_boxplot(width = if (feature == "Glycans") 0.74 else 0.58,
>                  outlier.shape = 16, outlier.size = 0.5, linewidth = 0.35) +
>     scale_x_continuous(breaks = NULL, expand = expansion(add = 0)) +
>     coord_cartesian(xlim = c(0.45, 2.55), clip = "off") +
>     scale_fill_manual(values = d_fill, breaks = c("Glycation", "Glycosylation"), drop = FALSE) +
>     labs(
>       title = unname(feature_labels[[feature]]), x = NULL,
>       y = if (show_y_title) "Detected per sample" else NULL, fill = "Glycan type"
>     ) +
>     d_theme
>   if (broken_axis) {
>     p <- p +
>       scale_y_continuous(
>         trans = broken_y_trans(), limits = y_limits,
>         breaks = c(1200, 1400, 1600, 1800, 6000, 6500, 7000, 7500),
>         labels = scales::label_number(big.mark = ","),
>         expand = expansion(mult = c(0, 0.03))
>       ) +
>       annotate("rect", xmin = 0.35, xmax = 0.55, ymin = 3000, ymax = 4800,
>                fill = "white", color = NA) +
>       annotate("segment", x = 0.38, xend = 0.52, y = 3100, yend = 3600,
>                linewidth = 0.35, color = "black") +
>       annotate("segment", x = 0.38, xend = 0.52, y = 4100, yend = 4600,
>                linewidth = 0.35, color = "black")
>   } else {
>     p <- p + scale_y_continuous(
>       limits = y_limits, breaks = scales::pretty_breaks(n = 3),
>       labels = scales::label_number(big.mark = ","),
>       expand = expansion(mult = c(0, 0.05))
>     )
>   }
>   p
> }

> relabel_grob <- function(g, mapping) {
>   if (is.null(g)) return(g)
>   lab <- tryCatch(g$label, error = function(e) NULL)
>   if (!is.null(lab) && all(trimws(lab) %in% names(mapping))) {
>     g$label <- mapping[trimws(lab)]
>     if (!is.null(g$gp)) g$gp$fontsize <- 13
>   }
>   if (!is.null(g$children)) {
>     for (nm in names(g$children)) g$children[[nm]] <- relabel_grob(g$children[[nm]], mapping)
>   }
>   if (inherits(g, "gtable")) {
>     for (i in seq_along(g$grobs)) g$grobs[[i]] <- relabel_grob(g$grobs[[i]], mapping)
>   }

More detail

Ethics Approval Check

The paper appears to analyse an open resource, which needs no ethics statement of its own.

We based this on:

Sample-level quality control followed the GTEx v8 protocol, removing outliers based on relative log expression, the D-statistic summarizing average correlation between sample pairs, and the Mahalanobis distance of each sample to a hierarchical clustering of samples.

Ethics statement:

The ROS and MAP studies were approved by an Institutional Review Board of Rush University Medical Center.

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.
