Targeted trans-glycoQTL mapping identifies long-range genetic regulation of the brain glycoproteome

To explore long-range genetic influences on protein glycosylation, we performed a targeted trans-glycoQTL analysis restricted to variants previously implicated in gene regulation by external genetic studies and databases (methods). We defined trans-glycoQTLs as associations in which the genetic variant and the gene encoding the target glycopeptiform were located on different chromosomes. Using a marginal screening threshold (MAF 0.05, P < ≥ ) followed by fine-mapping, this analysis nominated 1,183 credible sets involving 1,088 5 × 10 -8 glycopeptiforms from 435 glycoproteins (Fig. 6a).

We next investigated the trans-glycoQTL hotspot with the largest number of target glycopeptiforms. This locus, tagged by the missense variant rs11155297 in FUCA2, was associated with 31 glycopeptiforms from 12 glycoproteins (Fig. 6b). FUCA2 encodes ɑ-L-fucosidase 2, a member of glycoside hydrolase family 29 for which fucosidase activity has been reported, although its physiological substrates and contribution to glycan defucosylation remain uncertain 55 . The variant rs11155297 has previously been reported as a FUCA2 pQTL in plasma 56 . Consistently, our brain proteomic data also identified rs11155297 as a cis-pQTL for FUCA2 (p = ) (Fig. S6a). These findings suggest that genetic variation affecting 5.8 × 10 -25 FUCA2 abundance or activity may exert widespread effects on the brain glycoproteome.

Finally, we examined whether genes targeted by trans-glycoQTLs included established ADRD risk genes. Among the four ADRD risk genes identified as candidate trans-glycoQTL targets, APOD showed the strongest evidence for a long-range regulatory interaction (Fig. 6a). APOD glycopeptiforms were associated with five independent trans-glycoQTLs, including rs5848, an established ADRD risk variant located in the 3' untranslated region of GRN (Fig. 6c). The rs5848 variant is a strong cis-eQTL for GRN and was associated in trans with APOD protein abundance as well as six paucimannose APOD glycopeptiforms (Fig. 6d,e, S6b). Multi-omics colocalization supported a model with a shared causal variant underlying the ADRD GWAS signal, GRN eQTL in bulk tissue, excitatory neurons and oligodendrocytes, the APOD trans-pQTL, and six APOD trans-glycoQTLs, with rs5848 showing the highest variant colocalization probability within the 95% colocalization confidence set (Fig. 6f). In contrast, the remaining APOD glycopeptiforms showed little or no evidence of colocalization with rs5848 (Table S5, Fig. S6c). The corresponding GRN cis-pQTL was comparatively weak in our brain proteomic data (Fig. S6b), although rs5848 has previously been identified as a GRN cis-pQTL in plasma 57.

Thus, these targeted analyses demonstrate that trans-glycoQTL mapping can identify broad regulators of brain glycopeptiform abundance, as shown by FUCA2, and nominate disease-associated long-range regulatory interactions, as shown by the GRN-APOD locus.

Discussion

This study set out to investigate the effect of genetic variation on the human brain glycoproteome. We generated a novel glycoproteome dataset covering N-glycosylation (7,500 glycopeptiforms) and glycation (1,582 glycopeptiforms) in 316 human cortex samples with matched whole-genome sequencing data. In contrast to previous glycome studies [25] [26] [27] [28] , our site-specific glycoproteomic approach enabled us to investigate genetic effects on individual glycopeptiforms and glycosylation sites. We first characterized cis-glycoQTLs, defining a glycoQTL as a genetic locus associated with the abundance of a glycopeptiform. As expected, many glycoQTLs colocalized with pQTLs, indicating that genetic effects on glycopeptiform abundance may often be mediated through changes in total protein abundance. Their enrichment in regulatory regions further supports a contribution from gene regulatory mechanisms. However, many glycoQTLs remained significant after accounting for matched protein abundance, and glycosylated peptides were more frequently regulated by genetic variants than glycated peptides, suggesting that common genetic variation influences glycosylation beyond its effects on steady-state protein abundance.

Interestingly, glycoQTLs were enriched among high-mannose compared with complex or hybrid glycopeptiforms, indicating that genetic regulation may preferentially affect early steps of N-glycan biosynthesis in the ER, including co-translational glycan attachment and initial glycan processing. One possible explanation is that non-coding regulatory variants alter the rate of protein production or trafficking through the secretory pathway, thereby changing exposure to glycosylation enzymes without necessarily producing detectable changes in steady-state protein abundance. Consistent with this mechanism, previous studies have shown that co-translational processes, including translation kinetics, can affect N-linked glycosylation efficiency 58 . Our findings suggest that this concept extends to common human genetic variation and that regulatory variants can influence glycoproteoform composition through subtle changes in protein biogenesis rather than solely through changes in steady-state protein abundance.

After establishing a glycoproteome-wide cis-QTL map of the human brain, we integrated these data with ADRD GWAS summary statistics to identify glycoQTLs that are associated with disease risk. We found five genes with colocalized glycoQTL and ADRD risk signals, among which TMEM106B emerged as perhaps the most intriguing for further investigation. TMEM106B has been intensively studied in the context of frontotemporal dementia, but the locus has also been identified in recent ADRD GWAS 3, 4 . The ADRD risk variant has previously been associated with increased TMEM106B protein abundance 47 , whereas evidence for an eQTL effect is relatively weak. Recent independent studies demonstrated that a C-terminal fragment (CTF), spanning almost the entire luminal C-domain, can form amyloid-like fibrils that accumulate in the aging human brain [49] [50] [51] . The glycosite N183 detected in our dataset is located within this CTF and is of particular interest because the coding variant T185S, a candidate causal variant supported by our fine-mapping, directly alters its glycosylation sequon, converting the generally preferred N-X-T to N-X-S 48, 59 . Glycosylation at N183 has been confirmed by biochemical assays 46 and, more recently, visualized in brain-derived amyloid fibrils by cryo-EM 49 . However, the glycan compositions present at this site in the human brain and their relationship to T185S remained incompletely characterized 47.

Our mass-spectrometry approach identified six distinct glycan-associated mass shifts at N183, of which three, corresponding to HexNAc(2)Hex(5-7), could be quantified reliably for allele-specific analysis. Across these three glycan compositions, S185-containing glycopeptiforms showed lower measured abundances than their T185 counterparts. Particularly interesting was the variation in allelic ratios across glycan compositions within heterozygous donors: the strongest relative depletion of S185 was observed for the HexNAc(2)Hex(6)-carrying glycopeptiform. Although T185-and S185-containing glycopeptides could potentially differ in ionization or detection efficiency, our observation within heterozygous donors supports a glycan-dependent association between T185S and glycopeptiform abundance at N183. Thus, our work characterized the glycan compositions at N183, demonstrated glycan-dependent differences in allelic ratios, and prioritized the HexNAc(2)Hex(6)-carrying glycopeptiform for mechanistic investigation.

Given the reported aggregation of the TMEM106B CTF, we examined genetic associations at the peptide level. The risk-associated T185 allele was associated with higher abundance of a peptide mapping to the CTF core (C core ), whereas N-terminal peptides showed no significant association. This pattern suggests that the pQTL reflects CTF accumulation rather than a uniform change in full-length TMEM106B abundance 60 . The ADRD risk signal colocalized with both the HexNAc(2)Hex(6) glycoQTL and the C core pQTL, supporting a shared genetic basis for these molecular traits and disease susceptibility. The glycoQTL remained significant after adjustment for C core abundance, supporting an association with N183 glycosylation beyond variation captured by this peptide. These findings are consistent with a model in which T185-associated enrichment of the HexNAc(2)Hex(6) glycoform promotes CTF stability or aggregation. However, mediation analysis did not resolve the direction of this relationship. Alternatively, HexNAc(2)Hex(6)-carrying species could become enriched through preferential retention within the accumulating CTF pool. Our measurements also cannot distinguish glycopeptides derived from full-length TMEM106B versus cleaved CTF, or from soluble versus aggregated species. In summary, our glycoQTL study identified N183 glycan composition as a candidate molecular link between TMEM106B genetic risk and CTF accumulation, providing a target for mechanistic investigation.

In addition to TMEM106B, the ACE locus demonstrated that genetic associations with individual glycopeptiforms can diverge from those with total protein abundance. While the risk allele was associated with lower ACE protein abundance and, consistently, with lower abundances of most ACE glycopeptiforms, one colocalized glycopeptiform with a hybrid/complex glycan at N111 showed a comparatively modest QTL effect in the opposite direction. N111 is located within the N-terminal catalytic domain, which has been implicated in β-amyloid degradation 53, 54 . Although the site lies away from the catalytic center, structural studies have also identified glycan-glycan contacts at this site across the ACE dimer interface 61, 62 . However, the functional consequences of the observed glycosylation changes remain unknown.

Finally, we performed an exploratory trans-glycoQTL analysis to assess long-range genetic regulation of the brain glycoproteome. Since our sample size was modest for trans-QTL discovery, we adopted a targeted strategy and focused on the strongest associations. The trans-glycoQTL hotspot with the largest number of target glycopeptiforms was tagged by a missense variant in the fucosidase FUCA2, suggesting that the associated glycopeptiforms represent direct or indirect downstream targets of FUCA2. These findings provide human genetic evidence for a role of FUCA2 in glycoproteome regulation, while the underlying biochemical mechanism remains unresolved 55 . More generally, these results illustrate how larger future trans-glycoQTL studies could systematically identify the target proteins and precise glycosylation sites altered by glycan-modifying enzymes whose abundance or activity is under genetic control. Conversely, APOD emerged as the ADRD glycoprotein targeted by the largest number of independent trans-glycoQTLs. At the GRN risk locus, colocalization supported a shared genetic signal linking ADRD risk, GRN expression, APOD protein abundance, and six paucimannose APOD glycopeptiforms. This connection is biologically plausible given the involvement of both progranulin and APOD in lysosomal function 63 . Whether GRN mediates these trans-effects remains to be determined. A complementary analysis of this glycoproteomic resource identified colocalization between a QTL for an astrocyte transcriptomic program and multiple trans-glycoQTLs 64 , illustrating how this resource can help place tissue-level glycoQTLs within cell-type-resolved regulatory networks.

The broad glycopeptiform coverage provided by our mass-spectrometry approach is a major strength of this study, but the assay also has several limitations. First, despite the large number of quantified glycopeptiforms, these likely represent only a fraction of the molecular diversity of the human brain N-glycoproteome. Whether a glycopeptiform is detected depends on its abundance, enrichment efficiency, and mass-spectrometric detectability. Further, our enrichment strategy targeted only N-linked, not O-linked glycopeptides. Second, the glycoproteome was quantified at the tissue level, precluding direct assignment of glycoQTLs to specific cell types. Although glycoQTLs colocalized with eQTLs detected across all major brain cell types, the exact cellular origin of glycoQTLs remains unresolved. Differences in glycopeptiform abundance across cell types, combined with cell-type-specific genetic effects, could contribute to the divergent associations observed for different glycopeptiforms of the same protein at the tissue level. Third, our approach infers glycan composition but not glycan structure, limiting mechanistic interpretation of the functional consequences of altered glycosylation. In addition, peptide-level measurements do not reveal how glycans at different sites are combined on individual protein molecules, preventing reconstruction of intact glycoproteoforms.

Overall, we comprehensively characterized the genetic regulation of the human brain glycoproteome. By integrating site-specific glycoproteomics with proteomic, transcriptomic and genetic data, we demonstrated that common genetic variation shapes glycoproteoform composition, revealing a layer of genetic regulation not captured by protein abundance alone. These analyses further revealed molecular consequences of ADRD risk variants that would not be resolved by conventional transcriptomic or protein-level analyses, as illustrated by TMEM106B and ACE. As large-scale glycoproteomic datasets become available across tissues, cell types, and diseases, glycoQTL mapping should provide an additional molecular layer for understanding the functional consequences of human genetic variation. Figure 1. Study design and characterization of the human brain glycoproteome. a) Overview of the experimental design. Protein abundance and glycosylation (glycation and glycosylation) were quantified in DLPFC tissue from the ROSMAP cohort (n=316) alongside whole-genome sequencing (WGS) genotype data. QTL fine-mapping was performed to identify protein QTLs (pQTL) and glycoQTLs (glycoQTL). These QTL results, together with AD GWAS summary statistics and ADSP LD reference data, served as inputs for downstream integrative analyses, including mediation analysis to assess direct and indirect effects, TWAS to link genes and traits, stratified LD score regression (S-LDSC) for heritability enrichment, and colocalization analysis to evaluate shared genetic signals. b) Venn diagram showing the numbers of detected glycated and N-glycosylated glycopeptiforms. The intersection represents glycopeptiforms carrying both glycation and an N-linked glycan. c) Relative abundance of glycan classes among detected glycopeptiforms. d) Number of detected glycan compositions, glycoproteins, glycosites, glycopeptides, and glycopeptiforms per sample. Boxplots summarize the distribution across samples. C_core-adjusted abundances (gray). Points indicate effect estimates and error bars indicate 95% confidence intervals. e) Within-donor log₂(S185/T185) intensity ratios for HexNAc(2)Hex(5-7) in rs3173615 C/G heterozygotes. Points represent donors with both corresponding allelic glycopeptiforms quantified. The dashed line indicates equal S185 and T185 intensity. Brackets show unadjusted P values from two-sided paired t-tests comparing log₂ ratios between glycan compositions, using donors with both ratios available. f) Mediation analyses of rs3173615, with allele-summed HexNAc(2)Hex(6) abundance and C core abundance alternately specified as mediator and outcome. Total effects, indirect effects (ACME), and direct effects (ADE) are shown per S185/G allele with 95% confidence intervals. Online Methods ROSMAP cohort and neuropathologic characterization Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (DLPFC) tissue was obtained from 316 decedents of the Religious Orders Study (ROS) and Rush Memory and Aging Project (MAP), two longitudinal cohort studies of aging and dementia with harmonized clinical and postmortem data collection. 38 At enrollment, participants were free of known dementia and agreed to annual clinical assessments and brain donation at death. Participant characteristics are summarized in Table S1. QTL analyses were adjusted for quantitative measures of amyloid and tau pathology. β-amyloid burden was defined as the mean square-root-transformed β-amyloid load across eight brain regions, quantified by immunohistochemistry using the 4G8 antibody and digital image analysis as the percentage of tissue area positive for amyloid. PHF-tau tangle density was similarly defined as the mean square-root-transformed density of PHF-tau tangles across the same eight regions, quantified by AT8 immunohistochemistry and digital image analysis as tangle density (counts per mm²) 65 . The ROS and MAP studies were approved by an Institutional Review Board of Rush University Medical Center. All participants signed an informed consent form, an Anatomic Gift Act for brain donation, and a repository consent to allow their data and biospecimens to be shared.

Glycoproteomic data

Glycoproteomic profiling of dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (DLPFC) tissue from the ROS/MAP cohort was performed using a liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS)-based workflow. Briefly, tissue lysates were prepared by bead-beating, proteins were digested using the S-Trap method, and glycopeptides were enriched as described previously 39 . Glycopeptide mixtures were analyzed by nano-liquid chromatography (nanoAcquity, Waters) coupled to high-resolution tandem mass spectrometry (Orbitrap HF-X, Thermo Fisher Scientific) operated in data-dependent acquisition mode with stepped higher-energy collisional dissociation (HCD) fragmentation. Raw data were searched against the human proteome and a glycan library using Byonic 66 . Glycopeptide identifications were filtered at a 1% false discovery rate (FDR), followed by an additional minimum Byonic score threshold of 300 for increased stringency 67 . Glycopeptides were quantified using FlashLFQ with normalization and match-between-runs enabled and were subsequently log-transformed 68 . Glycopeptiforms with valid measurements in fewer than two-thirds of samples were excluded. Outlier samples with numbers of missing measurements above the 95th percentile of the missing-value distribution (3,275 missing values) were also excluded, removing 17 samples. The resulting dataset comprised 9,100 glycopeptiforms across 361 samples, including 316 samples with matched WGS and proteomic data. Manual review of noncanonical modification assignments identified two glycopeptiforms with modifications assigned to serine or threonine and 54 glycopeptiforms with putative W-linked glycans. Since these glycopeptiforms could not be confidently classified, the results reported here were restricted to the 9,044 glycopeptiforms carrying N-linked glycans or glycated lysines. For the QTL analyses, missing values were imputed using empirical Bayes matrix factorization implemented in flashier with point-Laplace priors and iterative backfitting for up to 300 iterations. The model was initialized using PCA components derived from a softImpute-completed matrix (rank = 50, λ = 30) 69 . For QTL analyses, we applied an additional filter to exclude potential artifacts caused by coding variants that alter peptide mass. Glycopeptiforms were excluded when (i) a coding variant located within the measured peptide was present in the 95% credible set and (ii) non-imputed glycopeptiform abundances followed the genotype-dependent pattern AA > AB > BB in the raw, unadjusted data or fewer than three BB donors were available to assess this pattern. Here, A denotes the allele matching the searched peptide sequence and B the alternative coding allele. This filter removed 21 glycopeptiforms. For TMEM106B, which was affected by this filter, we repeated the database search with both the T185 and S185 peptide sequences included. For each donor and glycan composition, we summed the normalized linear intensities of the corresponding T185-and S185-containing glycopeptiforms and then applied a log₂ transformation. If only one allelic glycopeptiform was quantified (for example, in homozygous donors), its intensity was retained. If neither was quantified, the combined feature was treated as missing. The resulting allele-summed TMEM106B features were included in the QTL analyses. N-linked glycans were classified into four classes based on glycan composition: paucimannose (truncated mannose-type glycans; HexNAc(2)Hex(1-4), with or without fucosylation), high-mannose (oligomannose-type glycans; HexNAc(2)Hex(≥5)), hybrid/complex (HexNAc(≥3)Hex(≥3), potentially with additional fucosylation and/or sialylation), and other (compositions not fitting these categories, including small or partially processed glycans). Hybrid and complex glycans were combined because their structures could not be distinguished based on glycan composition alone.

Proteomic data

Proteomic profiling of DLPFC tissue from the ROSMAP cohort was previously performed using tandem mass tag (TMT) isobaric labeling followed by liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry and has been described elsewhere 70, 71 . Peptide spectral matches were filtered at <1% FDR using Percolator implemented in Thermo Proteome Discoverer 72 . Peptides were grouped using parsimony, unique peptides were used for protein-level quantification, batch effects were mitigated using pooled reference channels and median polish normalization, and protein abundances were log 2 -transformed and median-centered. Proteins with more than 40% missing values were excluded, resulting in 7,389 proteins quantified across 596 ROSMAP participants, including 316 participants with matched glycoproteomic and WGS data. Missing values were imputed using empirical Bayes matrix factorization implemented in flashier with point-Laplace priors and iterative backfitting for up to 300 iterations, initialized using PCA components derived from a softImpute-completed matrix (rank = 50, λ = 30) 69 . The resulting imputed protein abundance matrix was used for downstream analyses.

Adjustment for protein abundance

To adjust glycopeptiform abundances for total protein abundance, glycopeptiforms were first mapped to proteins. For glycopeptiforms mapping to multiple proteins, the protein exhibiting the highest Pearson correlation with glycopeptiform abundance was selected. Of the 9,100 quantified glycopeptiforms, 134 mapped to multiple proteins, for which the candidate protein with the highest Pearson correlation was selected. Glycopeptiforms whose corresponding protein was not confidently quantified in the proteomic dataset (n=372) were excluded from the adjusted analyses. Protein adjustment was performed in the 354 samples with matched glycoproteomic and TMT proteomic data. For each glycopeptiform, glycopeptiform abundance was regressed on the abundance of the corresponding protein, and the fitted intercept plus the residuals were retained as the protein-adjusted abundance. The regression used imputed proteomic data and non-imputed glycoproteomic data. The resulting protein-adjusted matrix was subsequently imputed using the same low-rank matrix completion framework described above, based on softImpute (rank=50, λ=30) followed by empirical Bayes matrix factorization (flasiher) with point-Laplace priors and iterative backfitting (up to 300 iterations) 69 . A total of 316 participants with matched WGS data were included in the protein-adjusted analyses. Unless otherwise stated, all primary analyses used the unadjusted glycopeptiform abundances, whereas protein-adjusted abundances were used only for secondary analyses.

RNA data processing

Single-nucleus RNA sequencing (snRNA-seq) data from DLPFC tissue of 479 ROSMAP participants were generated and processed as previously described 10, 73 . Briefly, nuclei were profiled using the 10x Genomics Chromium platform, followed by doublet detection, cell-type annotation, and aggregation into donor-level pseudobulk expression profiles for six major brain cell types (excitatory neurons, inhibitory neurons, astrocytes, microglia, oligodendrocytes, and oligodendrocyte precursor cells). Expression values were normalized as counts per million (CPM) and subsequently quantile normalized. Bulk RNA-seq data from postmortem DLPFC tissue of 1,093 ROSMAP participants were generated as previously described 74, 75, 75 . For this study, gene-level expression was quantified with RNA-SeQC. Sample-level quality control followed the GTEx v8 protocol, removing outliers based on relative log expression, the D-statistic summarizing average correlation between sample pairs, and the Mahalanobis distance of each sample to a hierarchical clustering of samples. Genes with TPM below 0.1 in more than 20% of samples were excluded, and genes were further required to have at least 6 reads in at least 20% of samples. Counts were then normalized with TMM.

Genotype and Covariate processing

Whole-genome sequencing (WGS) libraries were prepared and sequenced as described previously 74 . Sequence reads were aligned to the GRCh38 reference genome, and variants were detected using the GATK pipeline 75, 76 . Project VCF files were processed with bcftools: multi-allelic sites were split and variants annotated with dbSNP, then filtered by FILTER status, genotype depth (DP ≥ 10), genotype quality (GQ ≥ 20) and allele balance, and monomorphic sites were removed. Variants were then converted to PLINK https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?SQqGx2 , followed by an additional round of QC. Samples with >10% missing genotypes were excluded, and variants deviating from Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium ( ) were removed. No minor allele frequency filter 𝑃 < 1 × 10 -8 was applied. The QC-passed BED file served as the genotype input for downstream analyses.

For each molecular phenotype, we used a common covariate set consisting of sex, age at death, study (ROS or MAP), the top 15 genotype principal components, estimated hidden factors, and two measures of AD pathology: β-amyloid load and PHF-tau tangle density. The hidden factors were estimated from the processed phenotype matrix by principal component analysis (PCA) 78 , with the number of retained components determined using the Marchenko-Pastur distribution.

Fine-mapping analysis

We performed genome-wide fine-mapping across unadjusted and protein-adjusted glycoproteomic and proteomic traits using SuSiE 40 , supplying covariate-residualized individual-level phenotype and genotype data. For each gene, we defined the analysis region as the union of the topologically associating domain (TAD) containing the gene 79 and a window extending approximately 1 Mb beyond the gene's transcription start and end sites, consistent with the FunGen-xQTL consortium fine-mapping framework 80 . We restricted fine-mapping to variants with a minor allele count (MAC) ≥ 5. For all molecular phenotypes, we applied an adaptive pre-screening step before running full fine-mapping using a single-effect SuSiE model (L = 1). Regions with insufficient evidence of genetic association were excluded from subsequent fine-mapping. This procedure yielded posterior inclusion probabilities (PIP) and 95% credible sets (CS) for each locus, and additionally generated cis-xQTL prediction weights for downstream TWAS analyses.

Fine-mapping results were post-processed to filter low-confidence credible sets. Specifically, we retained only credible sets meeting a minimum absolute coverage threshold of 0.8 and containing at least one variant with a posterior inclusion probability (PIP) > 0.025. Variants with a MAF < 0.05 were then removed from the resulting glycoQTL and pQTL credible sets.

TensorQTL association analysis

We performed genome-wide cis-xQTL association testing using TensorQTL 41 . For each molecular trait, we tested all variants within the TAD-based cis region defined above, restricted to those with a MAF ≥ 0.05, using the same individual-level genotypes as in the fine-mapping analysis. Nominal association statistics were obtained from a linear model of the molecular trait on variant dosage, with the covariate set described above included as fixed effects.

To assess trait-level significance while accounting for multiple testing across variants, we permuted phenotype values across individuals to generate a null distribution of the minimum nominal p-value within the cis window, and approximated this null distribution by a beta distribution to obtain a per-trait permutation P value. We then controlled the false discovery rate across traits and considered regional cis-associations significant at q < 0.05.

Glycoproteome-and proteome-wide association studies

We performed glycoproteome-wide and proteome-wide association studies (GlycoWAS and PWAS) to evaluate associations with ADRD risk, using the transcriptome-wide association study (TWAS) framework. Before model training, we restricted variants to MAF ≥ 0.05. For each molecular trait, we partitioned the genome into the same regions used for fine-mapping and trained prediction models for all genes whose transcription start sites fell within a given interval. At loci containing large numbers of variants, we sampled up to 8,000 variants per cross-validation fold to reduce computational burden. We used summary statistics from a recent ADRD meta-analysis https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?0mMBc6 , which were quality-controlled using allele harmonization and LD mismatch detection with SLALOM, excluding flagged variants.

We trained six models, including Elastic Net https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?i0jUVH , LASSO 82 , BayesR 83 , Bayesian LASSO 84 , Sum of Single Effects (SuSiE) 40 , and Mr.ASH 85 , to estimate cis-xQTL weights for each candidate gene. Elastic Net combines L1 and L2 penalties, whereas LASSO uses only the L1 norm. Bayesian LASSO places a double-exponential prior on variant effects, and BayesR places a mixture-of-normals prior with a point mass at zero, propagating uncertainty into the weight estimates. SuSiE models the effects of multiple causal variants using a probabilistic framework, and Mr.ASH fits a multiple regression with an adaptive shrinkage prior on variant effects. We assessed predictive performance by five-fold cross-validation, retaining genes imputable in at least one method (CV R² ≥ 0.01, CV P < 0.05) and selecting the model with the highest CV R². For each retained model, we computed a TWAS Z-score from the cis-xQTL weights, GWAS Z-scores, and LD matrices from the ADSP R4 European-ancestry LD reference partitioned into LD blocks defined in previous work 86 . A GlycoWAS or PWAS signal was considered significant if (1) the best prediction model yielded an association P value below the Bonferroni-corrected threshold, or (2) at least half of the prediction models yielded P values below this threshold.

Mendelian randomization

We performed Mendelian randomization (MR) using the credible-set-aware Wald-ratio approach, using fine-mapped glycoQTL and pQTL variants as instruments. For each molecular trait, defined as a glycopeptiform in the glycoproteome or a protein in the proteome, we matched instruments to the ADRD GWAS summary statistics by chromosome, position, and alleles, flipping the sign of the exposure effect when reference and alternate alleles were swapped, and derived GWAS effect sizes and standard errors from Z-scores, allele frequencies, and sample sizes under a standardized-phenotype model. We rescaled exposure effects to unit standard error. We grouped instruments by credible set and retained credible sets with cumulative PIP (cPIP) ≥ 0.5. Within each retained set, we computed a composite Wald ratio as the PIP-weighted mean of the per-variant ratios, with a delta-method standard error derived from the corresponding PIP-weighted second moment. We then obtained gene-level effects by fixed-effect inverse-variance-weighted meta-analysis across credible sets, assessing between-credible-set heterogeneity with Cochran's Q and I². Traits with no credible set meeting the cPIP threshold were reported as non-estimable. MR associations were considered significant when a molecular trait passed Bonferroni correction for the number of molecular traits tested (meta-P < 0.05 / N), showed strong causal support with a cumulative posterior inclusion probability (cPIP) of at least 0.7, contained at least one credible set, and showed no evidence of heterogeneity, defined by Cochran's Q P > 0.01 and I² < 0.4.

Trans-glycoQTL analysis

We compiled a target variant set from published large-scale QTL resources and from our FunGen-xQTL flagship analyses across molecular modalities 80 . We LD-pruned each resource separately (r² > 0.64, MAF > 0.01) using ROSMAP genotypes and merged the pruned sets with the ADRD variant list to form a combined panel. We tested candidate SNP-gene associations with TensorQTL https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?fKKR7c on covariate-residualized genotypes and molecular phenotypes, considering associations at . We assigned each SNP to an LD block by genomic coordinate 𝑃 < 5 × 10 -6 and annotated each block with its associated genes, yielding a block-gene map that constrained the search space for trans fine-mapping. We then fine-mapped each block with SuSiE 40 , using the LD block as the analysis window, to generate genome-wide trans-glycoQTL results. Among credible-set variants we retained those with MAF ≥ 0.05 and marginal association , computed from the univariate effect estimate and its standard error.

𝑃 < 5 × 10 -8

Colocalization analysis

We performed three multi-context colocalization analyses to evaluate genetic signals shared between glycoproteomic traits and other molecular traits or ADRD risk using ColocBoost 43 . Each colocalization event was summarized by a 95% colocalization confidence set for the shared causal variants, and a corresponding trait-sharing configuration specifying which traits shared this signal. First, we performed a genome-wide colocalization analysis, which jointly analyzed glycoproteomic and expression traits to identify cell-type-level cis-eQTL signals that colocalized with tissue-level glycoQTL for each gene. Second, we jointly analyzed glycoproteomic and proteomic traits mapped to the same gene, together with ADRD GWAS summary statistics, using the GWAS-prioritized mode. This analysis identified glycoQTL that colocalized with ADRD association signals genome-wide, thereby separating signals shared with the corresponding pQTL from glycopeptiform-specific effects undetected at the protein level. Third, at two loci prioritized by trans-glycoQTL mapping, we performed targeted cis-trans colocalization to characterize shared genetic signals underlying long-range molecular associations. At each locus, we jointly analyzed the prioritized trans-associated glycopeptiforms with cis expression and proteomic traits linked to the same variant; ADRD GWAS summary statistics were also included when the trans-glycoQTL was associated with ADRD risk. All three analyses used the TAD-based cis region defined for the fine-mapping analysis. The genome-wide analysis was restricted to variants with MAF ≥0.05, while targets TMEM106B, ACE, GRN/APOD used the default MAC ≥5 threshold. Consistent with the other analyses, we used the covariate-residualized individual-level phenotype and genotype data for molecular traits, and used the quality-controlled ADRD GWAS summary statistics and LD reference panel for analyses involving ADRD. ColocBoost was run with default settings, and we retained only colocalization events containing at least one variant with marginal . 𝑃 < 1 × 10 -4

Excess of overlap (EOO) analysis

We evaluated the enrichment of genomic annotations among significant fine-mapped variants (PIP > 0.1) within 95% credible sets using the excess-of-overlap framework described previously 87 . Variants in the baseline annotation panel served as the background, and significance was assessed using a leave-one-chromosome-out (LOCO) block jackknife framework. We compiled annotations from FILER 88 and represented them as binary indicators in variant-level annotation matrices. For each annotation, we computed an odds ratio contrasting annotated and unannotated variants by significance status, and an enrichment ratio defined as the proportion of significant variants falling within the annotation divided by the proportion of all background variants in that annotation. Because variants on the same chromosome are correlated through linkage disequilibrium, we recomputed both statistics 22 times, excluding one autosome at a time, and reported the mean log₂ odds ratio and mean enrichment ratio across replicates with jackknife standard errors derived from their between-replicate variability. We compared enrichment profiles across datasets using a two-sample z-test on the difference in enrichment, restricted to annotations with P < 0.05.

Heritability enrichment analysis

We used stratified linkage disequilibrium score regression (S-LDSC) implemented in the polyfun 89 package to quantify the contribution of our annotations to complex trait heritability, following the procedure described previously 87 . We encoded a binary annotation recording whether a SNP belonged to our significant glycosylation glycoQTL variant set, defined as fine-mapped variants with PIP > 0.1 and MAF ≥ 0.05 within 95% credible sets. The annotation was analyzed conditional on the 97-category baseline-LD v2.2 model, using LD scores computed from the ADSP reference panel and publicly available GWAS summary statistics for 27 traits [1] [2] [3] [4] 44, [90] [91] [92] [93] [94] [95] [96] . We report the standardized effect size , the proportionate change in per-SNP τ * heritability per one-standard-deviation increase in the annotation, with standard errors from the 200-block LDSC jackknife.

Mediation analysis

To test whether the genetic effect on TMEM106B protein abundance acts through glycosylation, we performed causal mediation analysis with the R package mediation 97 . For the target locus, we used the dosage of the Ser185 (G) allele as the exposure. In the forward model, the mediator was the abundance of the HexNAc(2)Hex(6) glycopeptiform and the outcome was the abundance of the C core peptide. In the switched model, C core was the mediator and the HexNAc(2)Hex(6) glycopeptiform was the outcome. For each direction, we fit a mediator model (mediator ~ exposure + covariates) and an outcome model (outcome ~ mediator + exposure + covariates), both adjusting for β-amyloid load, PHF-tau tangle density, neocortical TDP-43 pathology (presence or absence of cortical TDP-43 pathology determined by neocortical phosphorylated monoclonal TAR5P-1D3 staining), age at death, and sex. We estimated the average causal mediation effect (ACME), average direct effect (ADE), total effect, and proportion mediated, with 95% confidence intervals and P values from 5,000 nonparametric bootstrap replicates. Effects are expressed per Ser185 allele.
